      "text": "# Candidate B design\n\nAssignment: boundary-b. Input revision: fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73. Method: arena, candidate seat B. Owner: /root/boundary_b. Requested model/effort: inherit-parent. Observed model/effort: unknown. Design only.\n\n## Usage before types\n\nConsumers keep the stable facade. Construction and dependency injection are package internals. Each pricing instance captures one pure calculator; its batch method applies boundary validation before calling that calculator directly.\n\n### HTTP caller\n\n~~~ts\nimport { handle } from './index';\nhandle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}');\n// { status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' }\nhandle('{'); // { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }\n~~~\n\n### Migrated invoice consumer\n\n~~~ts\nimport { runBatch, type Order } from './index';\nexport function invoiceTotals(): number[] {\n  const orders: Order[] = [\n    { quantity: 2, unitPrice: 125 },\n    { quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 },\n  ];\n  return runBatch(orders); // [250, 99]\n}\n~~~\n\n### Package-internal isolated capability use\n\n~~~ts\nimport { total } from './domain/order';\nimport { createPricing } from './batch';\nconst invoicePricing = createPricing(total);\ninvoicePricing.runBatch([]); // []\ninvoicePricing.runBatch([{ quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 }]); // [99]\n// A separate instance may inject a pure calculator in a focused isolation test.\n// Production binds the mandatory domain total, once, without a setter.\n~~~\n\n## Derived types and signatures\n\nThese fenced sketches describe the final modules. Every operation body remains unimplemented. Construction/export expressions show dependency wiring only. Nothing here authorizes creating the source modules.\n\n### domain/order.ts\n\n~~~ts\nexport type Order = { quantity: number; unitPrice: number };\n/** Pure quantity * unitPrice. No parsing, validation, HTTP, state or I/O. */\nexport function total(order: Order): number {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe exact structural Order type remains intact. TypeScript number fields cannot encode positivity or integrality. Both input boundaries enforce those predicates at runtime. No brand, safe-integer restriction, extra-property rejection or richer money model changes the contract.\n\n### batch.ts\n\n~~~ts\nimport { total as domainTotal, type Order } from './domain/order';\ntype Calculator = (order: Order) => number;\nexport type Pricing = Readonly<{\n  total: Calculator;\n  runBatch: (orders: Order[]) => number[];\n}>;\n/** Private-to-package factory. Capture total once, with no replace/set API.\n * TODO: create a closure named runBatch(orders: Order[]): number[].\n * TODO: map orders; validate each at THIS batch boundary; then call total(order).\n * TODO: invalid order throws exactly Error('invalid order').\n * TODO: return an immutable pair { total, runBatch }; no JSON or HTTP dependency.\n * Each instance owns its closure and calculator, with no mutable registry/cache.\n */\nexport function createPricing(total: Calculator): Pricing {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n/** No setters; shared default has no mutable operational state. */\nexport const defaultPricing: Pricing = createPricing(domainTotal);\nexport const runBatch: (orders: Order[]) => number[] = defaultPricing.runBatch;\n/** Module-private narrowing; same integer/sign predicate as the legacy code. */\nfunction isBatchOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe parameter name total is deliberate. The batch closure calls its captured domain function directly after validation. It never calls a service forwarding method, HTTP adapter or serialization helper. Exporting its function reference preserves the runBatch entry point without an extra call frame. The factory owns the entire batch operation, rather than returning a shallow calculator wrapper.\n\n### adapters/http.ts\n\n~~~ts\nimport { defaultPricing } from '../batch';\nimport type { Order } from '../domain/order';\n// Private wire result. It is not an exported type or part of Pricing.\ntype HttpResult = { status: number; body: string };\n/** Parse raw JSON as unknown, catch parse errors only, narrow at this boundary.\n * TODO: malformed JSON -> { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }.\n * TODO: invalid order -> { status: 400, body: 'invalid order' }.\n * TODO: valid -> { status: 200,\n *   body: JSON.stringify({ total: defaultPricing.total(order) }) }.\n */\nexport function handle(raw: string): HttpResult {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\nfunction isHttpOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe HTTP adapter depends on the default pricing instance's domain capability. It never invokes runBatch. The dependency on batch.ts is a deliberate placement tradeoff: that required module is the capability owner as well as the batch boundary. No second composition module or cyclic static facade wiring is necessary. An injected calculator must satisfy purity by contract; TypeScript cannot prove purity.\n\n### index.ts\n\n~~~ts\nexport { handle } from './adapters/http';\nexport { runBatch } from './batch';\nexport type { Order } from './domain/order';\n~~~\n\n## Module map and dominant paths\n\n| Final path | Owner and dependencies |\n| --- | --- |\n| domain/order.ts | Exact Order shape and pure total. Imports nothing. |\n| batch.ts | Factory, immutable default capability and batch boundary. Imports domain only. |\n| adapters/http.ts | JSON, HTTP formatting and HTTP validation. Imports capability and domain type only. |\n| index.ts | Stable facade only. Exposes handle, runBatch and Order. |\n| consumer.ts | Invoice caller shown above, imports facade only. |\n| contract.test.mjs | Preserved runtime contracts; focused dependency checks added only during implementation. |\n| tsconfig.json | Retains existing strict noEmit checking. |\n| legacy.ts | Deleted at migration completion, with no compatibility replacement. |\n\nBatch: facade function reference -> batch-owned runBatch closure -> captured total. HTTP: facade export -> HTTP boundary -> captured defaultPricing.total. Both reach pure domain computation. Ignoring the facade re-export, each executable path needs at most three source modules. There is no collection index or cache to invent later; batch maps input order to output order and HTTP prices one validated order.\n\nValidation checks a non-null order, integer quantity > 0 and integer unitPrice >= 0. HTTP starts from parsed unknown. Batch receives Order[] but validates each element because it is an entry boundary. Keep the two local guards aligned with one table of valid/invalid boundary examples in tests. Their error policies differ. A general validator module would add indirection for a two-field predicate. Structural Order intentionally remains forgeable outside the boundaries.\n\n## Mandatory literal outcomes\n\n| Input/caller | Required outcome |\n| --- | --- |\n| handle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}') | { status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' } |\n| handle('{') | { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' } |\n| handle('null'), handle('{}'), zero quantity, negative price | { status: 400, body: 'invalid order' } |\n| runBatch([]) | [] |\n| invoiceTotals() | [250,99] |\n| runBatch([{quantity:0,unitPrice:125}]) | throws Error('invalid order') |\n\nPreserve integer validation for fractional, NaN and infinite fields as well. Removing the legacy batch JSON round trip deliberately changes access mechanics. Do not add serialization to mimic incidental toJSON/getter behavior. Target batch validation applies to the domain order itself. Large products retain ordinary JavaScript arithmetic and HTTP JSON.stringify behavior; no new numeric policy is proposed.\n\n## Eight red flags\n\n| Flag | Assessment and mechanism |\n| --- | --- |\n| Shallow module | Factory owns complete batch validation/error/calculation flow. Its total member also gives HTTP a pure domain capability; it is an alias rather than a forwarding method. Two callable members are enough. The construction seam remains extra cost for this tiny fixture. |\n| Information leakage | Pricing and Order contain domain functions/data only. HttpResult stays local to HTTP. The stable handle signature necessarily carries the mandated transport result, an explicit facade exception rather than a transport type re-export. |\n| Temporal decomposition | No parse/validate/compute pipeline modules. HTTP owns its whole boundary and batch owns its whole boundary. Domain owns arithmetic. |\n| Pass-through method | Static runBatch exports the captured closure by reference. total is the captured calculator by reference. No wrapper methods forward identical arguments. |\n| Split ownership | One closure owns each instance calculator; no setters or mutable shared registry. HTTP and batch own different input boundaries. Domain alone owns the production formula. |\n| Two ways to do one task | Production consumers import index only. Factory is a construction seam, never another external entry point. Delete legacy and RawOrder exports in the same migration. |\n| Importable internals | Residual TypeScript limitation: non-facade relative imports can compile. At implementation, include a Bun static dependency guard in the required verification gate: package consumers may import index only; HTTP may import defaultPricing and Order; index may export only the specified three names; tests alone may import createPricing. Fail the static gate on a new forbidden import/export. Do not claim index omission alone prevents deep imports or that ordinary tsc enforces this rule. |\n| Hand-synced list | No runtime feature registry. HTTP and batch guards repeat one predicate; the shared table of boundary behavior cases detects disagreement. Export allowlist is the facade contract itself, not a second list of business operations. |\n\n## Migration and verification plan\n\nThis candidate performs no migration. After coordinator synthesis and implementation authorization: preserve a pristine case-owned copy; run baseline installed-compiler and Bun contract checks; add domain and check it in isolation; switch batch factory and HTTP boundary; migrate facade and consumer; delete legacy; then require strict static checking, the Bun runtime contracts and dependency inspection together. Temporary import breakage is confined to named migration files in the owned implementation copy. No shared input is changed.\n\nThe final dependency inspection must prove batch contains no JSON.stringify, JSON.parse, handle reference or HTTP import; domain contains no HTTP or JSON; legacy and RawOrder have no references; defaultPricing captures the exported domain total; and both boundary guards retain integer/sign behavior. The Bun dependency guard is proposed work, not an executed check. No runtime or compiler pass is claimed by this design artifact.\n"

      "text": "# Candidate B rationale\n\nAssignment boundary-b. Input revision fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73. Method arena. Model/effort requested inherit-parent; observed unknown. Design only; no sibling output or rubric read.\n\n## Problem\n\nBatch currently serializes orders into HTTP and parses the response to recover multiplication. The fixed target requires pure total(Order), separate HTTP and batch validation, unchanged literal outcomes, stable facade names and legacy deletion. The private package has no external compatibility obligation. Grounding comes from all six committed migration files at the assigned SHA and boundary-design/how-return.md plus why-return.md. Historical operational motivation remains unknown.\n\n## Usage (caller's view)\n\nThe first section of design.md defines three call sites before the type sketch: handle(raw) through index, invoiceTotals through runBatch and Order, and a package-internal createPricing(total) instance. External callers never select a calculator or coordinate validation steps. They receive the existing HTTP result or numeric totals. The factory seam is for composition and isolation tests.\n\n## Shape\n\nKeep exact structural Order and pure total in domain/order.ts. An instance-owned factory in batch.ts captures an injected pure calculator and owns a complete validated runBatch closure. Its readonly total member gives HTTP access to that same calculator. The default instance binds domain total once; static runBatch exports its closure by reference. HTTP validates parsed unknown and formats its own transport result. This meets boundary-discipline without moving validation into the domain. Per foundational-thinking, arrays preserve batch ordering without an index. Per separate-before-serializing-shared-state, instances share no mutable state. Per make-operations-idempotent, repeated calls have no side effects or partial persistent state. Readonly capability members prevent rebinding at the type boundary; purity remains a semantic constraint. Per laziness-protocol and minimize-reader-load, the required batch module owns composition, avoiding another layer and cycles. Interface depth is moderate: two domain methods hide calculator ownership and the full batch boundary, while the stable facade still exposes only two operations and one type. It is deeper than a factory that merely wraps multiplication, but costs more construction knowledge than this fixture strictly needs. All eight red flags and the import-guard limitation are evaluated in design.md. Per encode-lessons-in-structure, boundary behavior examples and a static import check are proposed enforcement, with no implementation claimed.\n\n## Synthesis decision\n\nPending the coordinator's arena comparison. This seat recommends its coherent factory shape; it has neither inspected other candidates nor selected an arena base. No graft, independent judgment or model diversity is claimed.\n\n## Tradeoffs accepted\n\n- We accept a factory seam in exchange for isolated calculator ownership and tests without mutable global replacement.\n- We accept HTTP importing the capability owner in batch.ts in exchange for acyclic static facades and no extra composition module.\n- We accept two local predicates in exchange for boundary-local policy and shorter traces, with one behavior matrix to detect drift.\n- We accept a proposed static import gate in exchange for keeping internal construction exports out of the public facade. Plain TypeScript relative imports do not enforce package privacy.\n\n## Alternatives considered\n\nA factory that returns only total hides no meaningful behavior and leaves both callers to coordinate the rest; its interface depth is too shallow. A factory returning HTTP and batch handlers hides more boundary work, but exposes transport results on the capability and creates cyclic initialization when required modules also export default functions. A mutable service container hides wiring but exposes replace/reset ordering and shared-state hazards. Within this seat's factory direction, the immutable domain capability plus complete batch operation avoids those costs.\n\n## Open questions and risks\n\nWill the coordinator accept batch.ts as both capability owner and batch boundary, given HTTP's dependency on that module? Will the extra factory knowledge justify its injection seam for this small private package? Can implementation enforce the proposed import gate as part of static verification rather than relying on facade conventions? Will the behavior matrix include non-integer fields and large ordinary-number products so validation and JSON formatting remain deliberate? These are design review questions; literal target contracts require no additional user decision.\n\n## Next implementation step\n\nAfter synthesis and authorization, preserve the pristine case copy, record observed baseline checks, then introduce the pure domain Order and total and check that unit in isolation before changing either boundary.\n"
    },

      "text": "Architect the transport-independent pricing migration at exact task input revision fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73. Read /Users/digital-hawk/.codex/plugins/cache/created-by-me-remote/pstack-openai/0.1.0/skills/architect/SKILL.md plus full runner-prompt and rationale-template references and the linked principle leaves before applying. Two whole-shape seats: A direct static named modules with local boundary validators; B instance-owned pricing capability factory with injected pure calculator and static facade adapters. Both must satisfy required final paths and pure total. Discuss rejected alternatives within your own shape. Do not read sibling outputs or rubric. Sole writes your candidate directory. No external services/actions, installations, configuration, commits or other workers. Bun scripts. Return assignment ID, revision, method arena, paths, actual observed model/effort or unknown.\n\nCOMPLETE RUNNER PROMPT:\n# Architect runner prompt\n\nThe orchestrator passes this file through to every parallel candidate runner during Phase B and fills in the variable inputs around it: the task, the Phase A grounding artifacts, the isolated working directory, and the path to write outputs. The working directory is a git worktree when available, otherwise a per-runner subdirectory under the sketch dir. What matters is independence between candidates. Create isolated directories or worktrees only with authorization and observed capabilities, following the assignment contract. Without workers, use this discipline for sequential alternatives and disclose reduced capability; do not claim independence.\n\nYou are producing one candidate design in architect's parallel exploration. Read the **architect** skill in full first. That's the workflow you're inside. Output a candidate design package: type sketch, function signatures, module map, and prose rationale shaped per [`rationale-template.md`](rationale-template.md).\n\nApply the following discipline. The orchestrator compares candidates on these axes to pick a base.\n\n- Caller's usage first. Write the README-style usage and two or three real call sites before the types, then derive the type sketch from them. The usage is the spec. The two must agree, so reconcile the sketch to the usage, not the reverse.\n- Data structures first. Get the core types right and the code becomes obvious. Trace each dominant access pattern through the proposed structure. If the answer is \"we'll add a map / index / cache later,\" the structure is wrong.\n- Interface depth. Compare the capability hidden behind the public surface relative to the size of that surface. Prefer a simple interface that pulls complexity into the callee, even when the implementation becomes less simple. Do not put transport or wire types on the public API. Parse into domain types behind the interface.\n- Shared state: if two actors might both write, ask \"what happens?\" If the answer isn't \"nothing,\" default to per-actor state with a merge at the read boundary, per the **separate-before-serializing-shared-state** principle skill.\n- Make boundaries visible. `not implemented` errors for bodies, `// TODO` pseudocode for tricky logic, doc comments stating intent and invariants. A reader should trace data from input to output by reading types and signatures alone.\n- Encode invariants in types: hard-to-misuse types > runtime checks > prose comments, per the **encode-lessons-in-structure** principle skill.\n- Validate at boundaries, trust types inside, per the **boundary-discipline** principle skill. Business logic as pure functions. The shell stays thin.\n- Single source of truth per invariant. Derive instead of sync.\n- Idempotent state transitions where applicable, per the **make-operations-idempotent** principle skill. Ask what happens if the operation runs twice or crashes halfway.\n- Short call chains. If tracing the flow needs more than three files, flatten the hierarchy, per the **laziness-protocol** and **minimize-reader-load** principle skills.\n\nWith observed independent workers, you are one of the parallel runners. Different models are a goal, not an assumed fact. Record requested and observed models; unknown or identical observed models do not establish diversity. In a sequential fallback, you are producing one explicitly labelled sequential alternative. Produce the best design your model can make. Don't hedge against the others. Differences between candidates are the signal used to pick a base and graft. Converging on a safe-looking middle defeats the exploration.\n\nYou are seat A. Assignment boundary-a. Sole writable path /Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-evaluation-20261016/fixtures/durable-02/boundary-design/candidates/a. Requested model/effort inherit-parent."

      "text": "Assignment: `boundary-how`  \nInput revision: `fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73`  \nMethod: `how`, simple direct explainer; read-only inspection through `git show`. All six files under `committed-inputs/additional-v3/migration-end-state/` were read in full at that revision. Requested model/effort: inherit-parent. Observed model/effort: unknown.\n\n### Overview\n\nPricing currently lives entirely in `legacy.ts`. The HTTP handler owns JSON parsing, order validation, multiplication and response formatting. Batch pricing reaches that same calculation by serializing each order, calling the HTTP handler and parsing its response. `index.ts` is only a re-export facade. The named transport-independent domain is a required migration destination, not the current implementation. [legacy.ts:1–14; index.ts:1–2; target.md:5–12]\n\nThere is no persistent state, cache, database or service object in this package. Each call calculates from its arguments and returns a fresh result. [legacy.ts:2–14; consumer.ts:1–2]\n\n### Key concepts\n\n- **`RawOrder`** is a TypeScript shape with numeric `quantity` and `unitPrice`. It does not establish runtime validity. [legacy.ts:1,5]\n- **`handle(raw)`** accepts JSON text and returns `{ status, body }`. It currently combines transport and pricing responsibilities. [legacy.ts:2–6]\n- **`runBatch(orders)`** returns numeric totals, but currently obtains them through the HTTP-shaped path. [legacy.ts:8–13]\n- **`invoiceTotals()`** is a concrete internal consumer, supplying two orders through the stable facade and expecting `[250, 99]`. [consumer.ts:1–2; contract.test.mjs:9–12]\n\n### How it works\n\n```mermaid\nflowchart LR\n    H[\"HTTP caller: JSON text\"] --> F[\"index.ts: handle\"]\n    C[\"consumer.ts: invoiceTotals\"] --> B[\"index.ts: runBatch\"]\n    B --> S[\"legacy.runBatch: JSON.stringify each order\"]\n    S --> V[\"legacy.handle\"]\n    F --> V\n    V --> P[\"Parse JSON\"]\n    P --> Q[\"Validate quantity and unitPrice\"]\n    Q --> T[\"Multiply quantity × unitPrice\"]\n    T --> R[\"HTTP status and JSON body\"]\n    R --> H\n    R --> U[\"Batch checks status and parses total\"]\n    U --> C\n```\n\nFor HTTP, `handle` first parses JSON. A parsing exception returns exactly `{ status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }`. It then requires a non-null order, an integer quantity greater than zero and an integer unit price greater than or equal to zero. Failure returns exactly `{ status: 400, body: 'invalid order' }`. Success multiplies the two numbers and returns status `200` with a JSON body containing `total`. [legacy.ts:2–6]\n\nFor batch, `runBatch` maps over the orders. Each input becomes JSON text, passes through `handle`, and produces an HTTP-shaped result. A non-200 result becomes `Error('invalid order')`; success requires parsing the response body to extract its numeric total. An empty batch returns an empty array through ordinary `map` behavior. [legacy.ts:8–13; contract.test.mjs:9–12]\n\nConsequently, HTTP currently owns the effective runtime validation rules for both entry points. Batch owns its outward error translation, while also depending on HTTP serialization and response structure. [legacy.ts:5,10–12]\n\nThe required migration separates those responsibilities:\n\n| Responsibility | Current owner | Required final owner |\n|---|---|---|\n| Order shape | `legacy.ts`: `RawOrder` | `domain/order.ts`: `Order` |\n| Multiplication | `legacy.handle` | Pure `total(order)` in `domain/order.ts` |\n| HTTP parsing, validation and response | `legacy.handle` | `adapters/http.ts` |\n| Batch validation and errors | HTTP validation plus batch error translation | `batch.ts`, calling `total` directly |\n| Public exports | Re-exports from legacy | Stable `handle`/`runBatch`, exporting domain `Order` |\n\nThe final batch path must contain no input serialization, `handle` call or HTTP-response parsing. The domain must contain no HTTP or JSON concerns. Validation belongs at each entry boundary, while calculation belongs in the domain. [target.md:7–10]\n\n### Where things live\n\nAll paths below are relative to `committed-inputs/additional-v3/migration-end-state/` at the assigned revision.\n\n| File | What to inspect |\n|---|---|\n| `legacy.ts:1–14` | Entire current implementation and type |\n| `index.ts:1–2` | Stable facade and current legacy dependency |\n| `consumer.ts:1–2` | Internal batch caller |\n| `contract.test.mjs:4–12` | Literal HTTP responses, empty batch, consumer totals and invalid-batch error |\n| `target.md:3–16` | Package boundary, final tree, migration sequence and completion evidence |\n| `tsconfig.json:1` | Strict, no-emit static-check configuration |\n\n### Gotchas\n\n- The package is explicitly complete and private. There are no external consumers or compatibility requirements to justify retaining a legacy alias, wrapper or compatibility copy. `legacy.ts` must be deleted at completion. [target.md:3,12]\n- Intermediate import breakage is permitted only in the named migration files inside a case-owned copy. A pristine copy must remain available for reversal; shared inputs and unrelated sources must remain intact. [target.md:3,14]\n- Required sequencing is baseline checks, isolated domain introduction/checking, switching HTTP and batch, then legacy deletion and export cleanup. Temporary adapters do not need polishing. [target.md:14]\n- Existing validation checks integers and sign, but does not check safe-integer limits or explicitly reject extra properties. Those are observations of current code, not additional migration requirements. [legacy.ts:5]\n- Completion needs observed strict static checking using an installed compiler if available, observed Bun runtime contracts, and source inspection proving the final dependency directions and absence of legacy references. Bun transpilation does not satisfy type checking. [target.md:16; tsconfig.json:1]\n- This explanation inspected committed source only. Tests and the compiler were not executed, so their passing status is unknown."

      "text": "import { total, type Order } from '../domain/order';\nfunction isOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  return typeof value === 'object' && value !== null\n    && 'quantity' in value && typeof value.quantity === 'number'\n    && Number.isInteger(value.quantity) && value.quantity > 0\n    && 'unitPrice' in value && typeof value.unitPrice === 'number'\n    && Number.isInteger(value.unitPrice) && value.unitPrice >= 0;\n}\n\nexport function handle(raw: string): { status: number; body: string } {\n  let order: unknown;\n  try { order = JSON.parse(raw); }\n  catch { return { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }; }\n  if (!isOrder(order)) return { status: 400, body: 'invalid order' };\n  return { status: 200, body: JSON.stringify({ total: total(order) }) };\n}\n"

      "text": "import { total, type Order } from './domain/order';\nfunction isOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  return typeof value === 'object' && value !== null\n    && 'quantity' in value && typeof value.quantity === 'number'\n    && Number.isInteger(value.quantity) && value.quantity > 0\n    && 'unitPrice' in value && typeof value.unitPrice === 'number'\n    && Number.isInteger(value.unitPrice) && value.unitPrice >= 0;\n}\n\nexport function runBatch(orders: Order[]): number[] {\n  return orders.map(order => {\n    if (!isOrder(order)) throw new Error('invalid order');\n    return total(order);\n  });\n}\n"

      "text": "import {test,expect} from 'bun:test';\nimport {handle,runBatch} from './index.ts';\ntest('one acceptance corpus exercises both actual boundaries',()=>{\n  const valid=[{quantity:1,unitPrice:0},{quantity:2,unitPrice:125},{quantity:1,unitPrice:99,extra:true}];\n  const invalid=[null,{},[],true,1,'order',{quantity:0,unitPrice:1},{quantity:-1,unitPrice:1},{quantity:1.5,unitPrice:1},{quantity:1,unitPrice:-1},{quantity:1,unitPrice:1.5},{quantity:'1',unitPrice:2},{quantity:1,unitPrice:'2'}];\n  for(const order of valid){const total=order.quantity*order.unitPrice;expect(handle(JSON.stringify(order))).toEqual({status:200,body:JSON.stringify({total})});expect(runBatch([order])).toEqual([total]);}\n  for(const order of invalid){expect(handle(JSON.stringify(order))).toEqual({status:400,body:'invalid order'});expect(()=>runBatch([order])).toThrow('invalid order');}\n});\ntest('native batch invalid numbers and input immutability',()=>{\n  for(const number of [NaN,Infinity,-Infinity]){expect(()=>runBatch([{quantity:number,unitPrice:1}])).toThrow('invalid order');expect(()=>runBatch([{quantity:1,unitPrice:number}])).toThrow('invalid order');}\n  const order=Object.freeze({quantity:2,unitPrice:125});expect(runBatch([order])).toEqual([250]);expect(order).toEqual({quantity:2,unitPrice:125});\n});\n"

      "text": "FILE result.json\n{\n  \"assignmentId\": \"boundary-a\",\n  \"workerId\": \"/root/boundary_a\",\n  \"inputRevision\": \"fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73\",\n  \"resultRevision\": \"fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73\",\n  \"method\": \"arena\",\n  \"resultMethod\": \"arena\",\n  \"role\": \"candidate-seat-a-direct-static-modules\",\n  \"status\": \"completed\",\n  \"requestedModel\": \"inherit-parent\",\n  \"requestedEffort\": \"inherit-parent\",\n  \"actualModel\": \"unknown\",\n  \"actualEffort\": \"unknown\",\n  \"artifacts\": [\n    {\n      \"path\": \"boundary-design/candidates/a/design.md\",\n      \"sha256\": \"4d091177b2cd68936f22453adc0632a2cd9633d5438ee4fa1122597ec3ee38f3\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"path\": \"boundary-design/candidates/a/rationale.md\",\n      \"sha256\": \"d5875dfd722a5fec7e61c8f615e8f8688b73bad7d73177b4637153c3af713e24\"\n    }\n  ],\n  \"verified\": [\n    \"Read back both complete candidate artifacts\",\n    \"Checked all rationale headings\",\n    \"Checked all eight red-flag assessments\",\n    \"Checked caller-first section order\"\n  ],\n  \"limits\": [\n    \"Design only; no implementation or static/runtime checks\",\n    \"No sibling output or rubric reads\",\n    \"No arena synthesis or independent model-diversity claim by this seat\",\n    \"No todo tool; checklist in design.md\"\n  ],\n  \"sourceReads\": [\n    \"All six migration-end-state files via git show at inputRevision\",\n    \"boundary-design/how-return.md\",\n    \"boundary-design/why-return.md\",\n    \"architect full skill and runner/template/red-flags references\",\n    \"host/model/assignment/workflow references\",\n    \"linked principle leaves\",\n    \"arena and unslop skills\",\n    \"TypeScript skill and its full referenced patterns\"\n  ]\n}\n\nFILE rationale.md\n# Candidate A rationale\n\n## Problem\n\nBatch currently serializes orders, invokes HTTP, and parses a response to obtain multiplication. The committed target requires a transport-independent domain, stable `handle` and `runBatch`, literal response/error preservation, and deletion of legacy. The complete package is private; compatibility aliases have no justification. These constraints come from `target.md:3-16`, `legacy.ts:1-14` and the supplied how/why grounding at revision `fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73`.\n\n## Usage (caller's view)\n\n`design.md` starts with three caller examples before its type sketch. HTTP calls `handle(raw)` through `index.ts` and receives the same status/body. The migrated invoice consumer uses facade `runBatch` and domain `Order`; another batch caller gets `[]`, `[250]`, or the existing invalid-order exception without knowing JSON or HTTP conventions.\n\n## Shape\n\nDirect named modules own complete operations. `domain/order.ts` exports exactly `Order = { quantity:number; unitPrice:number }` and pure `total(Order):number`. HTTP parses and validates locally, batch validates locally, and both call total directly, per boundary-discipline. Local values and fresh arrays avoid shared writers, per separate-before-serializing-shared-state and make-operations-idempotent. The required plain numbers cannot encode integer/sign restrictions; guards enforce them, with no opaque brands that would alter caller construction, per type-system-discipline. A shared behavioral test corpus is the planned mechanism against validator drift, per encode-lessons-in-structure. The public operation count remains two, and each hides its full validation, errors and output handling. The mandated total function is shallow but pure; adding a service would not deepen the caller contract. Flat imports and export-only facade minimize layers, per laziness-protocol and minimize-reader-load.\n\n## Synthesis decision\n\nPending orchestrator comparison. This is seat A's direct-module candidate, not a selected base or a synthesized arena result. No sibling candidate or rubric was read; no grafts or cross-judge verdict are claimed.\n\n## Tradeoffs accepted\n\n- We accept two module-private validation predicates in exchange for local boundary ownership and a flat graph. One shared acceptance corpus must make divergence fail verification.\n- We accept runtime validity checks at each entry in exchange for the mandated plain structural `Order` and unchanged batch calling convention.\n- We accept temporary import breakage in a reversible owned implementation copy in exchange for deleting legacy without transitional compatibility code, per outcome-oriented-execution and subtract-before-you-add.\n- We accept ordinary JavaScript number behavior in exchange for literal contract fidelity. Exact arithmetic and tighter range policy would require a separate requirement.\n\n## Alternatives considered\n\nA shared validation pipeline with public parse/validate/calculate stages would hide less than it exposes: callers or entry modules would coordinate stages, and one order operation would span extra files. A public validated-order constructor would hide numeric checks, but force batch callers to learn construction and unwrap errors before pricing. A class with mutable current-order state would hide arithmetic behind methods while exposing lifecycle and concurrency rules; it adds caller obligations without useful capability. These rejected alternatives are within this static-module exploration; none claims knowledge of another seat's design.\n\n## Open questions and risks\n\nCan implementation require the shared behavioral corpus before completion so copied local predicates cannot silently diverge? Should a future requirement explicitly tighten large-number behavior, given that this migration preserves existing numeric checks and JavaScript multiplication? Neither question blocks this fixed-contract sketch or authorizes changing policy now.\n\n## Next implementation step\n\nAfter synthesis and separate implementation authorization, record baseline checks in the preserved owned copy, then introduce and check `domain/order.ts` in isolation.\n\nFILE design.md\n# Candidate A: direct named modules\n\nAssignment `boundary-a`; input revision `fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73`; method `arena`. Design only. Required package paths below are relative to the committed migration package, not this candidate directory.\n\n## Usage before types\n\nCallers keep importing the stable facade. HTTP callers supply JSON text; batch callers supply domain orders. Neither caller assembles validation and calculation stages.\n\n```ts\n// HTTP integration caller\nimport { handle } from './index';\nconst response = handle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}');\n// Exactly { status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' }\nconst malformed = handle('{');\n// Exactly { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }\n```\n\n```ts\n// Migrated consumer.ts\nimport { runBatch } from './index';\nimport type { Order } from './index';\nexport function invoiceTotals(): number[] {\n  // TODO: supply these orders to runBatch and return [250, 99].\n  // const orders: Order[] = [{ quantity: 2, unitPrice: 125 },\n  //                         { quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 }];\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n```\n\n```ts\n// Another batch caller, with runtime failure owned by runBatch\nimport { runBatch } from './index';\nconst emptyTotals = runBatch([]); // []\nconst totals = runBatch([{ quantity: 2, unitPrice: 125 }]); // [250]\n// runBatch([{ quantity: 0, unitPrice: 125 }]) throws Error('invalid order').\n```\n\n## Data and signatures derived from usage\n\nThe following blocks are declarations and unimplemented sketches. They are not source files to execute.\n\n```ts\n// domain/order.ts\nexport type Order = { quantity: number; unitPrice: number };\n/** Pure quantity * unitPrice. Trust boundary-validated Order, do not mutate it.\n * No parsing, HTTP concepts, validation, cache, or ambient dependencies. */\nexport function total(order: Order): number {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n```\n\n```ts\n// adapters/http.ts\nimport { total } from '../domain/order';\nimport type { Order } from '../domain/order';\n/** Module-private. Narrow unknown into Order using integer and sign checks. */\nfunction isOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n/** Parse once, validate once, calculate once, format response once. */\nexport function handle(raw: string): { status: number; body: string } {\n  // TODO: JSON.parse into unknown; catch only parse failures -> invalid json.\n  // TODO: use local isOrder; rejection -> invalid order.\n  // TODO: return status 200 and JSON.stringify({ total: total(order) }).\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n```\n\n```ts\n// batch.ts\nimport { total } from './domain/order';\nimport type { Order } from './domain/order';\n/** Module-private batch boundary narrowing; same acceptance rules as HTTP. */\nfunction isOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n/** Return one total per order in input order. Empty input returns [].\n * Throw Error('invalid order') on an invalid order; never return partial results.\n * Do not mutate inputs. No JSON, HTTP calls, or HTTP response handling. */\nexport function runBatch(orders: Order[]): number[] {\n  // TODO: map each order through local validation and direct total(order).\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n```\n\n```ts\n// index.ts, export map only\nexport { handle } from './adapters/http';\nexport { runBatch } from './batch';\nexport type { Order } from './domain/order';\n```\n\n`Order` has exactly the required structural shape. It does not statically prove positive integer quantity or nonnegative integer price. Each boundary supplies that runtime proof; the domain trusts it. Branding would change the required shape and force callers to construct opaque values. Empty batches need no stronger collection type. No schema library exists in the complete supplied tree; do not install one for these guards.\n\n## Module map and access patterns\n\n| Final path | Owns | Runtime dependencies |\n|---|---|---|\n| `domain/order.ts` | Order shape and multiplication | None |\n| `adapters/http.ts` | JSON parsing, local HTTP validation, response literals | `domain/order.ts` |\n| `batch.ts` | Local batch validation, totals array and batch error | `domain/order.ts` |\n| `index.ts` | Stable export selection | HTTP and batch named exports |\n| `consumer.ts` | Invoice order literals and batch invocation | `index.ts`; type-only `Order` |\n| `legacy.ts` | Nothing | Deleted, no replacement alias |\n\nHTTP access is facade, HTTP boundary, domain. Batch access is facade, batch boundary, domain. The consumer adds only the existing facade import. The facade owns no runtime decision and contains no forwarding function bodies. A reader can locate each operation's behavior in its boundary and `domain/order.ts`; no load/validate/transform service chain exists.\n\nValidation accepts integer quantity > 0 and integer unitPrice >= 0, as in `legacy.ts:5`. Guard unknown values through non-null object checks, property existence, numeric type checks, then integer/sign checks. Extra properties remain accepted. Do not add safe-integer or overflow rejection. Preserve JavaScript multiplication and HTTP JSON stringification behavior, including large-number behavior; the target requires `number`, not exact decimal arithmetic. Plain batch inputs are domain objects, not objects with transport serialization semantics.\n\nThe direct array traversal is the dominant batch access pattern. It requires neither an index nor a cache. Every result is call-local. Concurrent calls share immutable function definitions, not mutable data. A repeated call returns equivalent output for the same ordinary input; a failed batch exposes no partial output and writes no state.\n\n## Literal obligations\n\n| Input or entry | Required outcome |\n|---|---|\n| `handle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}')` | `{ status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' }` |\n| `handle('{')` | `{ status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }` |\n| `handle('null')`, `handle('{}')` | `{ status: 400, body: 'invalid order' }` |\n| HTTP quantity 0 or price -1 | `{ status: 400, body: 'invalid order' }` |\n| `runBatch([])` | `[]` |\n| `invoiceTotals()` | `[250,99]` |\n| Batch quantity 0 | Throws `Error('invalid order')` |\n\n## Migration and verification plan\n\n1. In an authorized case-owned copy, preserve the pristine committed tree and record baseline strict/compiler and Bun contract outcomes. This candidate does not create or edit that implementation copy.\n2. Introduce `domain/order.ts` with the required type and pure total. Check that unit in isolation before switching callers.\n3. Switch HTTP and batch to their named files and direct domain imports. Migrate consumer concepts. Temporary import breakage is limited to named migration files in the owned copy and reversible from pristine source.\n4. Delete legacy, clean facade exports and all references, then run the full strict compiler check through the already installed compiler if available and run the supplied contracts with Bun. Do not polish temporary wrappers.\n\nFor eventual implementation, supplement literal contracts with one shared test corpus applied to both entry boundaries: valid zero-price orders, extra properties, missing fields, null, fractional/negative/zero quantities, fractional/negative prices, and wrong primitive types. For non-JSON numeric values use native batch cases separately. This is the proposed structural guard against local-validator drift; it is not implemented or observed here. Assert boundary outcomes, not private helper bodies.\n\nInspect final sources for dependency directions, absence of JSON or HTTP concerns in domain/batch, absence of `RawOrder` and legacy references, and exact required paths. Do not claim a full static pass if no installed compiler exists. Bun transpilation alone is insufficient.\n\n## All eight red flags\n\n| Flag | Assessment and decision |\n|---|---|\n| Shallow module | `total` hides little because multiplication is the entire mandated domain today. Do not invent services around it. `handle` and `runBatch` each hide full entry validation/error behavior behind one call. |\n| Information leakage | Public `Order` is domain-only. HTTP's required raw string/result remains at `handle`; no exported wire type, parser, HTTP result type alias, or transport dependency enters the domain or batch. |\n| Temporal decomposition | Each boundary owns its complete operation. No separate parse/validate/format modules or callable public stages. |\n| Pass-through method | Facade has export declarations only. No `runBatch -> handle` or wrapper function that repeats arguments. |\n| Split ownership | Domain alone owns shape and arithmetic. HTTP and batch independently own their external failure forms. Numeric acceptance policy is intentionally copied locally; parity tests must detect divergence. No state has two writers. |\n| Two ways to do one task | Every batch total comes directly from `total`. Legacy and `RawOrder` are removed with all callers; there is no compatible alternate pricing route. |\n| Importable internals | Local validators are unexported, so named imports fail static checking. `total` and `Order` are intentionally available to package modules. A complete private package has no configured package export fence, so do not claim one prevents deep-path access. |\n| Hand-synced list | No registry or duplicated order/result type list. Local acceptance predicates are deliberate duplication; use one behavioral corpus for both rather than a second type/schema list. This risk remains until that verification exists. |\n\n## Workflow status\n\nNo todo tool is exposed; this checklist replaces that binding for this seat.\n\n- [x] Ground: read all six committed migration files and supplied how/why grounding.\n- [x] Sketch: caller-first direct-module candidate, types, map and rationale.\n- [ ] Agree: orchestrator compares independent candidates and chooses a synthesis.\n- [ ] Implement: outside this design-only assignment.\n- [ ] Scrap: conditional on a later demonstrated architectural failure.\n\nArena frame and fan-out were assigned by the orchestrator. This seat produces one candidate only. Cross-judge, pick, graft and synthesized verification belong to the orchestrator; no sibling outputs or rubric were read. Requested model/effort `inherit-parent`; observed model/effort `unknown`. No runtime or compiler checks were executed because no implementation was produced.\n"

      "text": "FILE result.json\n{\n  \"assignmentId\": \"boundary-b\",\n  \"workerId\": \"/root/boundary_b\",\n  \"inputRevision\": \"fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73\",\n  \"resultRevision\": \"fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73\",\n  \"method\": \"arena\",\n  \"resultMethod\": \"arena\",\n  \"seat\": \"B\",\n  \"status\": \"completed\",\n  \"requestedModel\": \"inherit-parent\",\n  \"requestedEffort\": \"inherit-parent\",\n  \"actualModel\": \"unknown\",\n  \"actualEffort\": \"unknown\",\n  \"artifacts\": [\n    \"boundary-design/candidates/b/design.md\",\n    \"boundary-design/candidates/b/rationale.md\"\n  ],\n  \"limits\": [\n    \"Design only; no implementation or runtime/static pass.\",\n    \"Candidate only; coordinator owns judging and synthesis.\",\n    \"No sibling or rubric read; no additional workers.\"\n  ]\n}\n\nFILE rationale.md\n# Candidate B rationale\n\nAssignment boundary-b. Input revision fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73. Method arena. Model/effort requested inherit-parent; observed unknown. Design only; no sibling output or rubric read.\n\n## Problem\n\nBatch currently serializes orders into HTTP and parses the response to recover multiplication. The fixed target requires pure total(Order), separate HTTP and batch validation, unchanged literal outcomes, stable facade names and legacy deletion. The private package has no external compatibility obligation. Grounding comes from all six committed migration files at the assigned SHA and boundary-design/how-return.md plus why-return.md. Historical operational motivation remains unknown.\n\n## Usage (caller's view)\n\nThe first section of design.md defines three call sites before the type sketch: handle(raw) through index, invoiceTotals through runBatch and Order, and a package-internal createPricing(total) instance. External callers never select a calculator or coordinate validation steps. They receive the existing HTTP result or numeric totals. The factory seam is for composition and isolation tests.\n\n## Shape\n\nKeep exact structural Order and pure total in domain/order.ts. An instance-owned factory in batch.ts captures an injected pure calculator and owns a complete validated runBatch closure. Its readonly total member gives HTTP access to that same calculator. The default instance binds domain total once; static runBatch exports its closure by reference. HTTP validates parsed unknown and formats its own transport result. This meets boundary-discipline without moving validation into the domain. Per foundational-thinking, arrays preserve batch ordering without an index. Per separate-before-serializing-shared-state, instances share no mutable state. Per make-operations-idempotent, repeated calls have no side effects or partial persistent state. Readonly capability members prevent rebinding at the type boundary; purity remains a semantic constraint. Per laziness-protocol and minimize-reader-load, the required batch module owns composition, avoiding another layer and cycles. Interface depth is moderate: two domain methods hide calculator ownership and the full batch boundary, while the stable facade still exposes only two operations and one type. It is deeper than a factory that merely wraps multiplication, but costs more construction knowledge than this fixture strictly needs. All eight red flags and the import-guard limitation are evaluated in design.md. Per encode-lessons-in-structure, boundary behavior examples and a static import check are proposed enforcement, with no implementation claimed.\n\n## Synthesis decision\n\nPending the coordinator's arena comparison. This seat recommends its coherent factory shape; it has neither inspected other candidates nor selected an arena base. No graft, independent judgment or model diversity is claimed.\n\n## Tradeoffs accepted\n\n- We accept a factory seam in exchange for isolated calculator ownership and tests without mutable global replacement.\n- We accept HTTP importing the capability owner in batch.ts in exchange for acyclic static facades and no extra composition module.\n- We accept two local predicates in exchange for boundary-local policy and shorter traces, with one behavior matrix to detect drift.\n- We accept a proposed static import gate in exchange for keeping internal construction exports out of the public facade. Plain TypeScript relative imports do not enforce package privacy.\n\n## Alternatives considered\n\nA factory that returns only total hides no meaningful behavior and leaves both callers to coordinate the rest; its interface depth is too shallow. A factory returning HTTP and batch handlers hides more boundary work, but exposes transport results on the capability and creates cyclic initialization when required modules also export default functions. A mutable service container hides wiring but exposes replace/reset ordering and shared-state hazards. Within this seat's factory direction, the immutable domain capability plus complete batch operation avoids those costs.\n\n## Open questions and risks\n\nWill the coordinator accept batch.ts as both capability owner and batch boundary, given HTTP's dependency on that module? Will the extra factory knowledge justify its injection seam for this small private package? Can implementation enforce the proposed import gate as part of static verification rather than relying on facade conventions? Will the behavior matrix include non-integer fields and large ordinary-number products so validation and JSON formatting remain deliberate? These are design review questions; literal target contracts require no additional user decision.\n\n## Next implementation step\n\nAfter synthesis and authorization, preserve the pristine case copy, record observed baseline checks, then introduce the pure domain Order and total and check that unit in isolation before changing either boundary.\n\nFILE verification.json\n{\n  \"assignmentId\": \"boundary-b\",\n  \"inputRevision\": \"fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73\",\n  \"method\": \"arena\",\n  \"checks\": [\n    \"All eight rationale sections present\",\n    \"Call sites precede type sketch\",\n    \"All eight red flags present\",\n    \"Artifacts read back in full\"\n  ],\n  \"scope\": \"Design artifact verification only; no implementation, compiler or runtime contract checks\",\n  \"sha256\": {\n    \"design.md\": \"b67c466337cd88f3e3c9abebb87ac905303501a914a0e083ef8d864e49e2211b\",\n    \"rationale.md\": \"8d7f41412060330e3f76fefba35642ed20250589084aebbc67eca9b9a10c506a\",\n    \"result.json\": \"c48a1b45c40e2d2919069a9c7244d0661dbf6cc640a54a33742872c0cc7afc6d\"\n  }\n}\n\nFILE design.md\n# Candidate B design\n\nAssignment: boundary-b. Input revision: fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73. Method: arena, candidate seat B. Owner: /root/boundary_b. Requested model/effort: inherit-parent. Observed model/effort: unknown. Design only.\n\n## Usage before types\n\nConsumers keep the stable facade. Construction and dependency injection are package internals. Each pricing instance captures one pure calculator; its batch method applies boundary validation before calling that calculator directly.\n\n### HTTP caller\n\n~~~ts\nimport { handle } from './index';\nhandle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}');\n// { status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' }\nhandle('{'); // { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }\n~~~\n\n### Migrated invoice consumer\n\n~~~ts\nimport { runBatch, type Order } from './index';\nexport function invoiceTotals(): number[] {\n  const orders: Order[] = [\n    { quantity: 2, unitPrice: 125 },\n    { quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 },\n  ];\n  return runBatch(orders); // [250, 99]\n}\n~~~\n\n### Package-internal isolated capability use\n\n~~~ts\nimport { total } from './domain/order';\nimport { createPricing } from './batch';\nconst invoicePricing = createPricing(total);\ninvoicePricing.runBatch([]); // []\ninvoicePricing.runBatch([{ quantity: 1, unitPrice: 99 }]); // [99]\n// A separate instance may inject a pure calculator in a focused isolation test.\n// Production binds the mandatory domain total, once, without a setter.\n~~~\n\n## Derived types and signatures\n\nThese fenced sketches describe the final modules. Every operation body remains unimplemented. Construction/export expressions show dependency wiring only. Nothing here authorizes creating the source modules.\n\n### domain/order.ts\n\n~~~ts\nexport type Order = { quantity: number; unitPrice: number };\n/** Pure quantity * unitPrice. No parsing, validation, HTTP, state or I/O. */\nexport function total(order: Order): number {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe exact structural Order type remains intact. TypeScript number fields cannot encode positivity or integrality. Both input boundaries enforce those predicates at runtime. No brand, safe-integer restriction, extra-property rejection or richer money model changes the contract.\n\n### batch.ts\n\n~~~ts\nimport { total as domainTotal, type Order } from './domain/order';\ntype Calculator = (order: Order) => number;\nexport type Pricing = Readonly<{\n  total: Calculator;\n  runBatch: (orders: Order[]) => number[];\n}>;\n/** Private-to-package factory. Capture total once, with no replace/set API.\n * TODO: create a closure named runBatch(orders: Order[]): number[].\n * TODO: map orders; validate each at THIS batch boundary; then call total(order).\n * TODO: invalid order throws exactly Error('invalid order').\n * TODO: return an immutable pair { total, runBatch }; no JSON or HTTP dependency.\n * Each instance owns its closure and calculator, with no mutable registry/cache.\n */\nexport function createPricing(total: Calculator): Pricing {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n/** No setters; shared default has no mutable operational state. */\nexport const defaultPricing: Pricing = createPricing(domainTotal);\nexport const runBatch: (orders: Order[]) => number[] = defaultPricing.runBatch;\n/** Module-private narrowing; same integer/sign predicate as the legacy code. */\nfunction isBatchOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe parameter name total is deliberate. The batch closure calls its captured domain function directly after validation. It never calls a service forwarding method, HTTP adapter or serialization helper. Exporting its function reference preserves the runBatch entry point without an extra call frame. The factory owns the entire batch operation, rather than returning a shallow calculator wrapper.\n\n### adapters/http.ts\n\n~~~ts\nimport { defaultPricing } from '../batch';\nimport type { Order } from '../domain/order';\n// Private wire result. It is not an exported type or part of Pricing.\ntype HttpResult = { status: number; body: string };\n/** Parse raw JSON as unknown, catch parse errors only, narrow at this boundary.\n * TODO: malformed JSON -> { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' }.\n * TODO: invalid order -> { status: 400, body: 'invalid order' }.\n * TODO: valid -> { status: 200,\n *   body: JSON.stringify({ total: defaultPricing.total(order) }) }.\n */\nexport function handle(raw: string): HttpResult {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\nfunction isHttpOrder(value: unknown): value is Order {\n  throw new Error('not implemented');\n}\n~~~\n\nThe HTTP adapter depends on the default pricing instance's domain capability. It never invokes runBatch. The dependency on batch.ts is a deliberate placement tradeoff: that required module is the capability owner as well as the batch boundary. No second composition module or cyclic static facade wiring is necessary. An injected calculator must satisfy purity by contract; TypeScript cannot prove purity.\n\n### index.ts\n\n~~~ts\nexport { handle } from './adapters/http';\nexport { runBatch } from './batch';\nexport type { Order } from './domain/order';\n~~~\n\n## Module map and dominant paths\n\n| Final path | Owner and dependencies |\n| --- | --- |\n| domain/order.ts | Exact Order shape and pure total. Imports nothing. |\n| batch.ts | Factory, immutable default capability and batch boundary. Imports domain only. |\n| adapters/http.ts | JSON, HTTP formatting and HTTP validation. Imports capability and domain type only. |\n| index.ts | Stable facade only. Exposes handle, runBatch and Order. |\n| consumer.ts | Invoice caller shown above, imports facade only. |\n| contract.test.mjs | Preserved runtime contracts; focused dependency checks added only during implementation. |\n| tsconfig.json | Retains existing strict noEmit checking. |\n| legacy.ts | Deleted at migration completion, with no compatibility replacement. |\n\nBatch: facade function reference -> batch-owned runBatch closure -> captured total. HTTP: facade export -> HTTP boundary -> captured defaultPricing.total. Both reach pure domain computation. Ignoring the facade re-export, each executable path needs at most three source modules. There is no collection index or cache to invent later; batch maps input order to output order and HTTP prices one validated order.\n\nValidation checks a non-null order, integer quantity > 0 and integer unitPrice >= 0. HTTP starts from parsed unknown. Batch receives Order[] but validates each element because it is an entry boundary. Keep the two local guards aligned with one table of valid/invalid boundary examples in tests. Their error policies differ. A general validator module would add indirection for a two-field predicate. Structural Order intentionally remains forgeable outside the boundaries.\n\n## Mandatory literal outcomes\n\n| Input/caller | Required outcome |\n| --- | --- |\n| handle('{\"quantity\":2,\"unitPrice\":125}') | { status: 200, body: '{\"total\":250}' } |\n| handle('{') | { status: 400, body: 'invalid json' } |\n| handle('null'), handle('{}'), zero quantity, negative price | { status: 400, body: 'invalid order' } |\n| runBatch([]) | [] |\n| invoiceTotals() | [250,99] |\n| runBatch([{quantity:0,unitPrice:125}]) | throws Error('invalid order') |\n\nPreserve integer validation for fractional, NaN and infinite fields as well. Removing the legacy batch JSON round trip deliberately changes access mechanics. Do not add serialization to mimic incidental toJSON/getter behavior. Target batch validation applies to the domain order itself. Large products retain ordinary JavaScript arithmetic and HTTP JSON.stringify behavior; no new numeric policy is proposed.\n\n## Eight red flags\n\n| Flag | Assessment and mechanism |\n| --- | --- |\n| Shallow module | Factory owns complete batch validation/error/calculation flow. Its total member also gives HTTP a pure domain capability; it is an alias rather than a forwarding method. Two callable members are enough. The construction seam remains extra cost for this tiny fixture. |\n| Information leakage | Pricing and Order contain domain functions/data only. HttpResult stays local to HTTP. The stable handle signature necessarily carries the mandated transport result, an explicit facade exception rather than a transport type re-export. |\n| Temporal decomposition | No parse/validate/compute pipeline modules. HTTP owns its whole boundary and batch owns its whole boundary. Domain owns arithmetic. |\n| Pass-through method | Static runBatch exports the captured closure by reference. total is the captured calculator by reference. No wrapper methods forward identical arguments. |\n| Split ownership | One closure owns each instance calculator; no setters or mutable shared registry. HTTP and batch own different input boundaries. Domain alone owns the production formula. |\n| Two ways to do one task | Production consumers import index only. Factory is a construction seam, never another external entry point. Delete legacy and RawOrder exports in the same migration. |\n| Importable internals | Residual TypeScript limitation: non-facade relative imports can compile. At implementation, include a Bun static dependency guard in the required verification gate: package consumers may import index only; HTTP may import defaultPricing and Order; index may export only the specified three names; tests alone may import createPricing. Fail the static gate on a new forbidden import/export. Do not claim index omission alone prevents deep imports or that ordinary tsc enforces this rule. |\n| Hand-synced list | No runtime feature registry. HTTP and batch guards repeat one predicate; the shared table of boundary behavior cases detects disagreement. Export allowlist is the facade contract itself, not a second list of business operations. |\n\n## Migration and verification plan\n\nThis candidate performs no migration. After coordinator synthesis and implementation authorization: preserve a pristine case-owned copy; run baseline installed-compiler and Bun contract checks; add domain and check it in isolation; switch batch factory and HTTP boundary; migrate facade and consumer; delete legacy; then require strict static checking, the Bun runtime contracts and dependency inspection together. Temporary import breakage is confined to named migration files in the owned implementation copy. No shared input is changed.\n\nThe final dependency inspection must prove batch contains no JSON.stringify, JSON.parse, handle reference or HTTP import; domain contains no HTTP or JSON; legacy and RawOrder have no references; defaultPricing captures the exported domain total; and both boundary guards retain integer/sign behavior. The Bun dependency guard is proposed work, not an executed check. No runtime or compiler pass is claimed by this design artifact.\n"

      "text": "Assignment ID: `boundary-judge`  \nInput revision: `fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73`  \nMethod: `arena`  \nVerdict: **Choose A as the base, with limited verification grafts from B.**\n\nRead-only review covered the rubric, task, pristine target, how/why grounding, every artifact in both candidate directories, and the frozen eight-red-flags reference. No writes, implementation, compiler/runtime execution, external services, or additional workers.\n\n| Rubric, 0–2 each | A | B |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| Exact final tree/contracts and transport independence | 2 | 1 |\n| One owner per invariant; all eight flags screened | 1 | 1 |\n| Deep interface, small caller cost, no speculative layers | 2 | 1 |\n| Caller-first sketch, full rationale, credible migration | 2 | 2 |\n| **Total** | **7/8** | **5/8** |\n\nA follows the required dependency shape directly: each boundary imports `total`, the facade contains exports only, and legacy disappears. B preserves the visible literal outcomes in its sketch, but introduces calculator construction, capability ownership, and HTTP-to-batch coupling without a demonstrated requirement.\n\n| Red flag | Candidate A | Candidate B |\n|---|---|---|\n| Shallow module | Mandated arithmetic is shallow; complete boundary operations hide useful validation and error handling. Appropriate minimal shape. | Factory hides batch behavior, but calculator capture and `total` exposure add little capability. Extra interface relative to this stateless task. |\n| Information leakage | Domain `Order` and arithmetic remain transport-free. Required HTTP signature stays at its boundary. | Wire result stays local. However, HTTP learns that the calculator is owned by the batch capability, exposing composition knowledge across boundaries. |\n| Temporal decomposition | Complete boundary operations; no public stage pipeline. | Also avoids public stage decomposition. Combining composition with batch ownership is a separate concern. |\n| Pass-through method | Export-only facade; direct domain calls. | Function-reference aliases avoid forwarding bodies. They still add indirection, so “no extra frame” does not establish useful depth. |\n| Split ownership | No shared mutable state. Numeric acceptance policy is duplicated between guards; proposed parity verification remains necessary. | No shared mutable state. Same duplicated acceptance policy, plus injected calculators create additional formula authorities outside the production default. |\n| Two ways to do one task | One arithmetic function; legacy deletion removes the old path. | Default facade and exported `createPricing` provide two construction/use routes. Test-only intent is not enforced by the current sketch. |\n| Importable internals | Guards are private; domain exports are intentional. Correctly acknowledges absence of a package fence. | Factory, capability type and default instance are exported. Proposed import gate is useful but unimplemented; calling these exports “private-to-package” does not make them inaccessible. |\n| Hand-synced list | Two guard predicates repeat acceptance rules. Shared corpus helps detect divergence but remains proposed work. | Same repeated predicates. Proposed export/import allowlists add maintenance obligations and must be tied to actual contract/dependency checks. |\n\n**Interface depth:** A offers the same simple caller operations while hiding each complete boundary’s work. Its small domain function is mandated by the task; wrapping that function would not improve depth. B keeps public facade usage simple, but package maintainers must understand a factory, calculator parameter, capability object, default binding and export aliases. No mutable state, variable production calculator, or lifecycle requirement justifies that additional knowledge.\n\n**Exact-contract conflicts and limits:**\n\n- B’s `runBatch` invokes an injected `Calculator`. The default binds the domain function, but the exported factory also permits batch instances that do not call the required domain `total`. This weakens the unconditional requirement that batch call `total` directly.\n- B’s HTTP adapter imports `defaultPricing` from `batch.ts` and calls its member. It can reach the same domain function by reference, but departs from the straightforward required boundary-to-domain ownership. Treat this as a contract ambiguity requiring revision, not proof that literal HTTP tests fail.\n- Extra named exports are not expressly forbidden by the target’s wording. B’s factory exports should therefore be rejected for unnecessary surface and weakened enforcement, rather than falsely described as an explicit export-list violation.\n- Neither candidate demonstrates an incorrect literal outcome. Neither implements or verifies those outcomes.\n- Both retain duplicated validation predicates. Their shared behavioral corpus must become an actual completion check; the documents alone do not establish invariant enforcement.\n\n**Concrete synthesis:**\n\n- Keep A’s exact domain signature, direct HTTP/batch imports, local validators, export-only facade, legacy deletion and four-phase migration.\n- Graft B’s explicit dependency-verification requirements and its distinction between proposed enforcement and observed checks.\n- Use B’s concrete invoice caller example to make A’s consumer sketch immediately reviewable.\n- Require one shared boundary acceptance corpus, with separate native batch cases for non-JSON numeric inputs. Preserve existing integer/sign and ordinary-number behavior.\n- Reject B’s factory, `Pricing`, `defaultPricing`, injected production calculator seam and HTTP dependency on batch.\n- Do not graft a general import-policy framework merely to police those rejected construction exports. Scoped source/dependency checks suffice for the selected small package.\n\nBoth candidates report observed model/effort as unknown; this comparison establishes independent candidate review, not model diversity."

    {
      "path": "fixtures/durable-02/boundary-design/outcome-review.md",
      "originalExternalPath": "/Users/digital-hawk/Herd/pstack-workflow-evaluation-20261016/fixtures/durable-02/boundary-design/outcome-review.md",
      "sha256": "33400d792a32b6dbc3d0d94053407ba1386f99f412bbabf0f6804bdda54e077e",
      "bytes": 8350,
      "owners": [
        "grades-native-durable-02.json#1"
      ],
      "text": "Read-only outcome review for input SHA `fb7d9f00a700b1f6c3c88204d967433d99c11a73`. This review assesses selection and implementation quality; it assigns no case grade or host status. I inspected recorded execution results without rerunning the implementations.\n\n### arena-walkthrough\n\nSelecting A is supported by the actual code. Its entry retains the mounted value during disposal, then invalidates after reporting settles. Repeated teardown returns the same completion promise, and synchronous or asynchronous report failures reach the caller through rejection. B preserves the original value through a captured reference but invalidates before deferred reporting, which misses the literal temporal requirement. The judge explicitly recognized that distinction instead of treating both successful probes as equivalent evidence.\n\nThe synthesis coherently keeps A’s ownership and completion policy. The graft is a hand-adapted remount-before-disposal test from B; it does not introduce B’s immediate invalidation or uncaught microtask error policy. Rejecting a global map and timer-based deletion follows the demonstrated ownership and scheduling failure.\n\nVerification is substantive: the probe checks pinned vendor bytes against the committed input and digest, deferred execution, repeated teardown, independent same-key lifetimes, invalidation and failure handling. Both synthesis and graft verification records show exit `0`. These establish the exercised local behavior, not compatibility with an arbitrary disposer. A vendor that never calls back, or reporting that never settles, can retain the entry indefinitely. Callers must handle teardown rejection.\n\nThe native order supports a fresh later judge: B and A terminate at orders `25` and `26`, parent inspection follows at `29–30`, and the distinct judge starts at `31`. Judgment completes at `37`, before synthesis at `43`.\n\nThe exploration’s breadth is constrained: the coordinator assigned both whole shapes in advance. This is a comparison of those alternatives, not evidence that the workers independently discovered the design space.\n\n### candidate-cross-review\n\nThe selected A implementation directly satisfies the required lifecycle ordering. Its entry owns value retention, the actual deferred disposer and completion. The distinct-value overlap probe is particularly useful: it demonstrates separate ownership beyond two callbacks that both report `true`.\n\nB is meaningfully different from A and from walkthrough B: it retains an explicit caller-owned map with identity-guarded invalidation. That guard prevents an older mount from deleting a newer value, but does not restore an older active value after the newest mount unmounts. It also reports after invalidation and exposes more coordination surface. Those are concrete reasons to prefer A. The judge’s rejection of B’s immediate invalidation, map plumbing and uncaught report-error policy is consistent with the implementation.\n\nThe synthesis preserves A and grafts coverage of both same-key teardown orders plus remount-before-disposal. It avoids combining incompatible ownership policies. Recorded synthesis and graft probes exit `0`, including reproduction of the committed original failure, vendor pinning, asynchronous lifetime retention and cleanup after report errors.\n\nThe selected implementation assumes the pinned disposer’s scheduling behavior. Its `subscription.dispose()` call sits outside a scheduling-failure cleanup path; a synchronous scheduling exception would leave its completion unresolved. The supplied vendor normally queues a microtask, so the existing report-failure checks do not establish this additional behavior.\n\nThe separate trials are honestly bounded:\n\n- Native evidence records the unavailable-worker spawn rejection, `agent thread limit reached`. No worker artifact is substituted for that rejection.\n- The dropout worker actually executes the authorized failure command and exits `7`. Its `failure.json` records no candidate and excludes integration. The worker completes its report while the task assignment remains blocked.\n- The survivor contains a real lifecycle proposal, implementation, rationale and probe. Its parent-check record exits `0`. It remains one survivor, with no competitive selection, judge or trial synthesis claimed.\n\nMain candidates terminate at orders `50` and `54`; parent inspection occurs at `55–56`; the fresh judge starts at `57`, completes at `60`, and synthesis follows at `62`. The failure exercises remain separate from that main comparison.\n\n### boundary-design\n\nA is the better fit for the stipulated package. Its boundaries call the pure domain function directly, the facade contains export declarations, and no construction mechanism is needed for this stateless calculation. B’s factory, injected calculator, capability object and HTTP dependency on `batch.ts` add maintenance knowledge without a demonstrated production variation or lifecycle requirement. The judge carefully distinguishes unnecessary surface and ambiguous dependency compliance from an invented literal-test failure.\n\nBoth candidates provide caller-first examples, module ownership, complete rationale sections and all eight red-flag assessments. The comparison acknowledges duplicated acceptance policy rather than claiming that private validators automatically create one invariant owner.\n\nThe implementation follows the selected architecture:\n\n- `domain/order.ts` contains the required structural type and pure multiplication.\n- HTTP owns parsing, validation and response formatting.\n- Batch validates and calls `total` directly.\n- The facade exports `handle`, `runBatch` and domain `Order`.\n- The consumer uses domain orders; `legacy.ts` is absent.\n\nThe useful grafts are B’s concrete invoice example and scoped dependency verification. The synthesis rejects B’s factory rather than adding an import-policy framework to police an abstraction it did not retain.\n\nVerification goes beyond transpilation. Recorded baseline, isolated-domain and final compiler executions use installed TypeScript with strict, no-emit configuration and exit `0`. Final Bun execution records four tests, zero failures and 49 assertions. The shared boundary corpus exercises both actual boundaries, while native batch checks cover non-JSON numeric values and frozen-input immutability. Source inspection supports the required dependency directions.\n\nTwo evidence-quality gaps remain:\n\n- The synthesized documents retain copied candidate-stage language, including unchecked workflow steps and statements that verification is proposed or unobserved, despite the completed implementation and recorded checks. The implementation evidence is stronger than the final narrative’s consistency.\n- `no-legacy-references.json` scans only domain and batch. It supports their dependency restrictions, but its filename overstates the search scope. My inspection of the remaining implementation files found no legacy dependency.\n\nNative ordering supports terminal inspection before fresh judgment: candidates finish at `69` and `74`, inspection occurs at `76`, the judge starts at `77` and finishes at `81`, and synthesis/implementation follows at `82`. No additional checkpoint was requested. No observed design failure required a scrap cycle.\n\n### Shared evidence limits\n\n`evidence/native/index.json` identifies distinct native worker threads, all observed as `gpt-6.1-sol` with medium effort. Requests inherit the parent; this supports worker independence, not model diversity.\n\nReceipt `method` and `resultMethod` objects are coordinator normalization of one observed artifact-producing attempt. Original worker returns say `arena`; they are not native structured method objects. Pending owner reservations were resolved after spawn and should remain distinguishable from final observed identities.\n\nThe assignment-event label `completed-and-inspected` conflates terminal completion with later inspection. Separate native observations show inspection before every judge, so the label is imprecise without reversing the relevant ordering.\n\nThe requested installed `0.1.0` orchestration-contract path is currently absent. I read its archived copy at `evidence/source/references/orchestration-contract.md`. That supports review against the preserved contract, not current installed discovery."
    },
